[Journal]
Storage=persistent
Compress=yes
SystemMaxUse=1GB
SystemKeepFree=1G
SystemMaxFileSize=50M
MaxRetentionSec=3month
Approved configuration updated for your plan: 1 GiB of persistent journal storage, with a
three-month retention target. We'll also preserve a straightforward way to export readable
logs for your GitHub repository.
1. What format are Linux logs stored in?
Systemd journal — binary, structured records
Stored under /var/log/journal/ once persistence is enabled. These are not
normally encrypted; they contain structured fields, timestamps, priorities and
message text. Use journalctl or GNOME Logs to read them.
GitHub upload — plain-text .txt
We can export the journal to readable UTF-8 text, including timestamps and NVIDIA
kernel messages. That is suitable for GitHub and for sharing the evidence here.
One important distinction: 1 GiB is the journal's storage budget, not a single file we should
upload to GitHub. GitHub has a 100 MiB per-file limit for ordinary Git repositories, and
focused diagnostic exports are generally more useful anyway.
2. Apply the configuration
Run this on Linux Mint — PC in a terminal. It backs up the target configuration if one already
exists, creates the persistent journal directory, and applies the settings you approved.
Systemd conventionally uses 1G to express one gibibyte; that is the value I'll use rather than
1GB .
The commands change journal storage and retention only. They do not alter NVIDIA or suspend settings.
echo '=== BACK UP EXISTING DROP-IN, IF PRESENT ==='
CONF=/etc/systemd/journald.conf.d/10-persistent.conf
if sudo test -f "$CONF"; then
sudo cp -a "$CONF" "$CONF.bak.$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
fi
echo
echo '=== CREATE PERSISTENT JOURNAL DIRECTORY ==='
sudo install -d -o root -g systemd-journal -m 2755 /var/log/journal
sudo systemd-tmpfiles --create --prefix /var/log/journal
echo
echo '=== WRITE APPROVED CONFIGURATION ==='
sudo install -d -o root -g root -m 755 /etc/systemd/journald.conf.d
sudo tee "$CONF" >/dev/null <<'EOF'
[Journal]
Storage=persistent
Compress=yes
SystemMaxUse=1G
SystemKeepFree=1G
SystemMaxFileSize=50M
MaxRetentionSec=3month
EOF
echo
echo '=== RESTART JOURNAL SERVICE AND FLUSH ==='
sudo systemctl restart systemd-journald
sudo journalctl --flush
echo
echo '=== VERIFY EFFECTIVE SETTINGS ==='
systemd-analyze cat-config systemd/journald.conf
echo
Review the output before proceeding. In particular, confirm the drop-in appears in the
effective configuration, the service reports active , and the journal now has persistent
storage. The current boot may still be the only listed boot until we reboot; don't reboot just
to test this yet.
3. Export readable logs for GitHub
Once persistence is verified, this command creates a focused plain-text export of NVIDIA,
DRM, suspend/resume and related kernel messages from the current boot.
The export is plain text and can be opened in a text editor or uploaded to your GitHub
repository. Because it filters the kernel log, it may omit relevant messages from the display
manager or user session; we can export a wider incident window if necessary.
Privacy note: inspect the file before uploading it publicly. Logs may contain usernames,
device identifiers, network addresses or other system details.
After you've run the configuration commands, paste the verification output. I'll check that
persistent logging is actually active before we move on to the next NVIDIA wake-up test.
echo '=== VERIFY STORAGE ==='
journalctl --disk-usage
sudo ls -ld /var/log/journal
journalctl --list-boots
echo
echo '=== VERIFY JOURNAL SERVICE ==='
systemctl is-active systemd-journald
OUT="$HOME/nvidia-kernel-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S).txt"
sudo journalctl -b -k -o short-iso-precise --no-pager |
grep -Ei
'NVRM|nvidia|nouveau|drm|Xid|GPU|PM:|suspend|resume|ACPI|watchdog|lockup|pan
ic|oops|fallen off the bus' |
tee "$OUT"
echo
echo "Saved text log: $OUT"
ls -lh "$OUT"
file "$OUT"

This installs it at your requested location with owner-only access and execution permission.
Run the diagnostic
Linux Mint — Dell Precision M4800
The script may ask for your sudo password to read protected system logs.
What it collects
Current and previous boot kernel messages, when available.
NVIDIA driver version, GPU identification and DRM errors.
Suspend/resume, ACPI and display-manager messages.
High-priority system errors.
Relevant portions of /var/log/kern.log and /var/log/syslog .
Journal storage usage and available boot history.
The report is saved automatically to /home/laco/Downloads/check-after-crashYYYYMMDD-HHMMSS.txt . It is plain text, with permissions restricted to your account by
default.
Important: the script is diagnostic only. It does not modify your driver, journal configuration
or power settings. Review the report for private information before uploading it to GitHub.

# Linux Mint — collect post-crash diagnostics

My conclusion so far: the NVIDIA graphics stack is a suspect, but we haven't proved it
caused the failed wake-up. The missing evidence is what happened during the transition back
from S3.
What the evidence tells us
Confirmed: legacy NVIDIA driver
Your Dell Precision M4800 uses an NVIDIA Quadro K2100M, and the logs identify the
proprietary NVIDIA driver as 470.256.02.
Suspicious, but not conclusive: graphics initialization
The logs contain NVIDIA/DRM initialization messages and failures involving
/dev/dri/card0 . These are worth investigating, but they don't establish that the GPU
failed to resume from sleep.
Unconfirmed: the actual wake-up failure
The screen stayed black, the mouse had power, and the keyboard appeared unresponsive.
That is consistent with a graphics-resume problem, but could also involve the kernel,
ACPI, or another device. USB power alone doesn't prove the system resumed successfully.
Missing: previous-boot journal
journalctl -b -1 couldn't retrieve the previous boot because persistent journaling
wasn't available. We therefore lack the most valuable evidence from the failed wake-up
itself.
My working diagnosis
The NVIDIA 470 driver interacting with the Quadro K2100M and the modern Linux Mint 22
kernel is a reasonable first hypothesis. The driver's age makes compatibility worth examining,
but age alone doesn't prove a bug, and we shouldn't change drivers without stronger
evidence.

Ah, that secureupdate output is important. It changes how I would approach the
investigation: we need to distinguish the driver that is loaded now from the NVIDIA
drivers that are installed or built for other kernels.
And your memory about a power-state or wake-up issue is plausible. A docked laptop can still
encounter a GPU resume problem; being docked does not guarantee that the graphics card is
fully powered or reinitialized correctly after sleep.
1. What your update report actually proves
The key point: your report does not show that the update forced NVIDIA 580 into the running
kernel. It explicitly confirms that 470.256.02 was loaded both before and after the APT
upgrade.
Check Result Meaning

Loaded NVIDIA
driver
470.256.02 470 is active now.

DKMS

kernel.
NVIDIA 580
DKMS
Installed for 6.8.0-139 ; built
for 6.8.0-147
A 580 module exists in the DKMS build state, but
that does not mean it is loaded.
APT upgrades None This run did not upgrade packages.
The 580 entry is still worth investigating. A module being built by DKMS is not the same thing
as the system selecting it at boot. The installed packages, module aliases, initramfs contents,
and boot configuration can affect which driver gets selected.
2. About the 470-versus-580 issue
Your rule is clear: keep NVIDIA 470; do not switch to 580. We will treat 470 as the required
driver unless you explicitly decide otherwise.
Ubuntu's documentation explains that DKMS can build modules for individual kernels, while
driver packages and boot-time module selection determine what is actually used.
Ubuntu Des… +1
So, for now, don't remove either version, reinstall drivers, or rebuild the initramfs. Let's
inspect which files and packages the machine is actually using first.
3. Read-only investigation — next step
Run this on Linux Mint, in your normal terminal. It makes no configuration changes.
LINUX MINT — host terminal
echo "========== ACTIVE NVIDIA MODULE =========="

cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version
modinfo -F version nvidia
modinfo -n nvidia
echo
echo "========== INSTALLED NVIDIA PACKAGES =========="
dpkg-query -W -f='${db:Status-Abbrev} ${binary:Package} ${Version}\n' \
'nvidia*' 'libnvidia*' 2>/dev/null
echo

apt-cache policy nvidia-driver-470 nvidia-driver-580
echo

cat /proc/driver/nvidia/params 2>/dev/null |
grep -iE 'power|suspend|preserve|rmLogonRC|enable'
echo
echo "========== NVIDIA MODULES IN CURRENT INITRAMFS =========="

laco@Unknown:~$
Haha, fair enough — your manual update is now the prime suspect! 😄 But there's an
important distinction in your command.
What probably happened
Your command runs several operations in sequence:
1. apt-get update refreshes package indexes. It does not install packages.
2. apt-get upgrade upgrades installed packages. It can install new dependencies, but
ordinarily does not remove installed packages.
3. flatpak update updates Flatpak applications, not APT-managed NVIDIA drivers.
4. apt-get autoremove --dry-run previews packages considered automatically
installed and no longer required. It doesn't remove anything.
5. You only proceed with autoremove if you answer y when packages are proposed for
removal.
The key detail: if the NVIDIA 580 packages were already marked as manually installed or
required by package dependencies, autoremove would not necessarily remove them.
Conversely, your apt-get upgrade could potentially have installed or upgraded NVIDIArelated dependencies, depending on the package state at the time.
What your history proves
Your output shows an APT transaction on November 1 at 03:20, with the command recorded
as apt-get upgrade . It doesn't show the full transaction contents, and the displayed history
doesn't establish when the 580 packages were installed.
So we shouldn't yet conclude that yesterday's update reinstalled 580. We need to inspect the
actual package history.
One targeted, read-only check
Run this to see what the November 1 upgrade actually changed:
Then check the detailed package-manager log for NVIDIA changes:
sudo zgrep -h -A 25 -B 2 \
'Start-Date: 2026-11-01 03:20:29' \
/var/log/apt/history.log*
sudo zgrep -h -E \
' (install|upgrade|remove|purge) (nvidia-|libnvidia-)' \
/var/log/dpkg.log*

ChatGPT said:
Could the forced power-down have restored NVIDIA 580?
Very unlikely by itself. A forced power-down can interrupt an update or package installation
if one is happening at that moment. However, it does not normally trigger APT to reinstall an
older or newer NVIDIA driver branch during the next boot.
Your archived conversation is particularly useful here. It documents that:
The Quadro K2100M requires your known-good NVIDIA 470.256.02 setup.
The 580 branch was previously purged because it was unsuitable for your hardware.
Driver 470 was installed using NVIDIA's official .run installer with DKMS integration.
GitHub
Three different mechanisms to distinguish
1. Forced shutdown
Can interrupt a package transaction or leave filesystem changes incomplete. It does
not independently choose NVIDIA 580 and reinstall it.
2. Kernel updates and DKMS
When a new kernel is installed, DKMS can rebuild registered modules for that kernel. It
does not normally install a different NVIDIA branch merely because a kernel update
occurred. Community … +1
3. APT package management
This is where the 580 packages most likely entered the picture: an installation,
dependency resolution, or earlier package-management action. Your November 1 APT
history does not show the 580 branch being installed during that recorded upgrade.
Ubuntu Serv… +1
Why are the 580 packages still there?
Your apt-mark results provide a useful clue:
Package branch APT status
NVIDIA 470 Marked manually installed
NVIDIA 580 Marked automatically installed
NVIDIA 535 Marked automatically installed
You said:
“Automatically installed” means APT considers a package to have been installed as a
dependency, rather than explicitly requested as a manually installed package. It does not
mean the package is automatically activated at boot.
Official documentation: Ubuntu — NVIDIA driver installation and package removal and
Ubuntu — unnecessary packages and autoremove . These explain the relationship between
driver branches, DKMS, and APT's automatic/manual package tracking. Ubuntu Serv… +1
One final read-only check
Before we blame a kernel recovery mechanism, let's find out which installed packages depend
on the 580 driver and why APT might be retaining it.
The second command only simulates removal; it doesn't uninstall anything.

remains unproven. Your active driver is still 470.256.02, so let's preserve that working
configuration while we identify why APT retains the other branches.

There are two separate questions here, and your new APT output reveals something
important:
1. What NVIDIA officially documents for driver 470.256.02.
2. Whether 470.256.02 is a sensible choice for your Quadro K2100M on kernel 6.8.0-147.
My initial recommendation is to preserve the working 470 driver while we investigate the
package situation. I would not switch to 580 simply because APT has installed its packages.
1. Your APT metadata has an anomaly
Your output shows a chain of dependencies that should not be casually accepted as normal:
nvidia-dkms-470-server
→ nvidia-dkms-535
→ nvidia-dkms-580
The installed 470 packages are held, and the 470.256.02 packages come from Ubuntu Noble
updates/security. Yet their dependencies point to newer driver branches.
That is a package-metadata issue worth investigating—not evidence that the 580 kernel
module is currently active. Your loaded module remains 470.256.02.
2. What NVIDIA officially says
NVIDIA Quadro K2100M
470 is the relevant legacy branch
Kepler architecture
NVIDIA's official documentation establishes three relevant points:
470 is the last Linux legacy driver branch for Kepler GPUs. NVIDIA explicitly says that
470 is the last branch supporting Kepler. NVIDIA +1
470.256.02 is still NVIDIA's latest published 470 Linux driver. It remains listed on
NVIDIA's Unix driver archive. NVIDIA
The release you linked dates from June 4, 2024. Its release notes list CUDA 11.4, OpenGL
4.6, Vulkan 1.2 and OpenCL 3.0. NVIDIA Docs
One important qualification: your link is the Tesla/Data Center release notes, not the Quadro
notebook-specific compatibility document. It is useful for understanding the 470 release, but
it does not certify your M4800/K2100M combination.
Also, NVIDIA's support documentation distinguishes Quadro notebook GPUs from desktop
Quadro products. The notebook product support history is documented separately. NVIDIA +1
3. Linux kernel 6.8.0-147 + driver 470.256.02
Here is my assessment:
nvidia-driver-470-server
→ nvidia-driver-535
→ nvidia-driver-580
Item Assessment
Quadro K2100M Kepler; 470 is the relevant legacy branch
Driver 470.256.02 Latest published 470 release
Kernel 6.8.0-147 Your installed module is currently built and loaded
The NVIDIA release notes do not explicitly certify kernel 6.8.0-147. However, we have direct
evidence that your installed 470.256.02 module is running on that kernel.
My recommendation: keep kernel 6.8.0-147 and driver 470.256.02 together for now.
I would not upgrade the kernel just to chase a newer version, and I would not replace 470 with
535 or 580.
4. Something important I discovered about Ubuntu's packaging
This is particularly relevant to your 580 problem.
Ubuntu deliberately retired its 470 packages in 2025, turning them into transitional packages
pointing to 535. That is exactly why your installed 470-server package declares a
dependency on 535.
However, following hardware compatibility regressions, Ubuntu subsequently restored the
regular 470 package in version:
470.256.02-0ubuntu0.24.04.6
The changelog explicitly says "Restore upstream version 470.256.02", referring to Ubuntu
bug 2155202. Ubuntu Disc… +2
Your installed 470-server package is version .5 , the transitional release—not the restored
regular .6 package.
The package situation

Redirects to 535. This explains the unexpected dependency.
470 regular .6 Restored
Ubuntu restored the actual 470.256.02 package in October 2026.
535 / 580 Not suitable for your target
DKMS compatibility Working in the current installation, based on the module evidence
Suspend/resume Not guaranteed by the driver version alone
Future kernel upgrades Potential compatibility risk

1. Preserve the working 470.256.02 kernel module and its current operation.

1. What your latest output proves
Confirmed The 580 DKMS source belongs to nvidia-dkms-580 .

2. Why it is built for kernel 147
The most likely sequence is:
1. The 580 package was installed.
2. DKMS registered its source.
3. Kernel 6.8.0-139 received an installed 580 module.
4. When kernel 6.8.0-147 became available, DKMS attempted to build 580 for it.
5. The build succeeded, but the installed 470 module remains the active driver.

The issue you encountered with Linux Mint is caused by a hardware
driver mismatch: the **NVIDIA 580.x driver series dropped support
for Kepler-architecture Quadro GPUs** (such as the Quadro
K2100M). Modern, rapidly updating distributions (like Ubuntu and
Mint) push new kernel builds and current NVIDIA driver branches,
breaking legacy DKMS kernel modules.
For the Dell Precision M4800, the highest official, productiongrade proprietary driver supported by NVIDIA is the **legacy
470.xx series** (or 390.xx depending on the exact chip revision).
Moving to a distribution with LTS (Long Term Support) kernel
management, enterprise DKMS stability, or curated legacy drivers
prevents this broken update loop.
---
### Recommended Linux Distributions Chart
| Rank | Distribution | Primary Strengths | Stability (Quadro
K2100M) | Security Maintenance | Legacy Hardware Compatibility |
Recommended Driver Approach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1** | **Debian 12 (Bookworm)** | Rock-solid stability; frozen
kernel tree; native legacy repo packages. | **9.8 / 10** | **9.5 /
10** | **9.8 / 10** | Official non-free nvidia-legacy-470xxdriver package. |
| **2** | **Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) 9** *(or Rocky Linux 9 /
AlmaLinux 9)* | Enterprise-grade target platform; stable 5.14 LTS
kernel backports. | **9.5 / 10** | **9.8 / 10** | **9.2 / 10** | kmodnvidia-470xx via ELRepo repository. |
| **3** | **openSUSE Leap 15.6** | YaST hardware configuration
tool; automated system rollback snapshots. | **9.1 / 10** | **9.3 /
10** | **9.0 / 10** | Dedicated NVIDIA repository (G05 legacy driver
branch). |
| **4** | **Ubuntu 22.04 LTS (Jammy Jellyfish)** | Fixed kernel
standard; explicit legacy 470 driver pinning via APT. | **8.5 / 10** |
**9.0 / 10** | **8.8 / 10** | Locked nvidia-driver-470 package
via APT hold. |
| **5** | **MX Linux 23** | Lightweight Debian base; dedicated
NVIDIA driver installer utility. | **8.8 / 10** | **8.8 / 10** | **9.5 /
10** | Built-in MX Driver Installer targeting legacy branch. |
---
### Technical Breakdown & Recommended Distributions
Section Title: Primary Recommendation - Debian 12 (Bookworm)
Debian 12 is the optimal distribution for the Dell Precision M4800.
Unlike rolling-release distributions or desktop distros that force
modern GPU stack metapackages, Debian keeps kernel releases
frozen during its stable lifecycle.
* **Kernel Model:** Fixed LTS kernel (no unexpected version jumps
breaking DKMS builds).
* **Legacy Driver Integration:** The official non-free repositories
maintain dedicated legacy packages (nvidia-legacy-470xxdriver) built specifically to avoid contamination from newer
5xx/6xx driver branches.
* **Desktop Option:** Can be paired with XFCE, Mate, or
Cinnamon for a light footprint on 4th Gen Haswell architecture.
Section Title: Enterprise Alternative - Enterprise Linux 9 (Rocky
Linux / AlmaLinux)
Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9 and its binary-compatible rebuilds
(Rocky Linux or AlmaLinux) are engineered for workstation
hardware lifecycle stability.
* **Stability & Security:** Utilizes kernel backporting rather than
major kernel version bumps, ensuring driver module compatibility
remains intact over long maintenance windows.
* **ELRepo Support:** Third-party driver management via ELRepo
provides pre-compiled kmod-nvidia-470xx kernel modules,
eliminating DKMS compilation failures during routine system
updates.
Section Title: Desktop Utility Alternative - openSUSE Leap 15.6
openSUSE Leap uses the core base of SUSE Linux Enterprise (SLE).
* **Rollback Capabilities:** Features Snapper and Btrfs root
filesystem integration out of the box, allowing you to instantly roll
back system state via GRUB if a package update ever affects
display initialization.
* **Hardware Management:** YaST handles hardware detection
and pins the correct legacy branch automatically without pulling in
incompatible branches.
---
### Preventing NVIDIA Driver Breakage on Legacy Hardware
Regardless of the distribution chosen, running a legacy
workstation GPU requires sticking to two key administrative
guidelines:
* **Display Server:** Always select an **X11 (Xorg)** desktop
session. Legacy 470.xx series drivers lack full Wayland protocol
implementation, which leads to black screens, unresolvable
greeter crashes, or fallback to software rendering.
* **Avoid Modern metapackages:** Avoid metapackages like
nvidia-driver without version qualifiers, as these automatically
resolve to modern release branches (such as 580/600 series) that
completely drop support for Kepler-generation Quadro hardware.

# Technical Handover Document
## 1. Original objective
* Primary Goal: Resolve a broken NVIDIA display driver state
following a kernel update on a Dell Precision M4800 running Linux
Mint Cinnamon, restoring dual-monitor output to an external 28"
monitor.
* Success Criteria: Completely remove residual or non-functional
NVIDIA drivers (including driver 580 series), cleanly install NVIDIA
Display Driver version 470.256.02, compile the module into the
current running Linux kernel via DKMS, and verify active display
output on both the internal and external 28" monitor.
* Scope: Execute driver purging, dependency installation, official
NVIDIA runfile execution (`NVIDIA-Linux-x86_64-470.256.02.run`),
and display manager restarts via terminal without requiring
GRUB/advanced boot menu interventions.
## 2. Environment and constraints
* Hardware:
* Laptop: Dell Precision M4800 workstation.
* GPU: NVIDIA Quadro K2100M.
* Docking Station: Dell E-Port Replicator PR03X.
* Displays: Internal laptop panel and external Samsung 28" 4K
monitor connected via dock display outputs.
* Operating System: Linux Mint Cinnamon (64-bit architecture,
Kernel Linux 6.x series).
* Software Dependencies: `build-essential`, `dkms`, `linuxheaders-$(uname -r)`, NVIDIA legacy driver installer (`NVIDIALinux-x86_64-470.256.02.run`).
* Display Manager: LightDM (`lightdm.service`).
* Operational Constraints:
* The system must use DKMS integration to build kernel modules
dynamically.
* Display manager must be explicitly stopped (`systemctl stop
lightdm`) prior to running the standalone `.run` installer to avoid X
server conflicts.
* Static configuration forcing NVIDIA as primary
(`/etc/X11/xorg.conf.d/10-nvidia.conf`) and driver 470 legacy
support must be preserved for hardware stability.
## 3. Work performed and evidence
* Purged Old Drivers [Verified]: Executed `sudo apt-get purge
"*nvidia*" -y` followed by `sudo apt-get autoremove --purge -y`
and `sudo apt-get clean`. Explicitly removed broken remnants of
the 580 driver series.
* Installed Build Tooling [Verified]: Executed `sudo apt update` and
`sudo apt install build-essential dkms linux-headers-$(uname -r) -y`
to ensure kernel header alignment.
* Downloaded & Executed NVIDIA 470.256.02 Installer [Verified]:
Downloaded `NVIDIA-Linux-x86_64-470.256.02.run` from official
NVIDIA servers, assigned execute permissions (`chmod +x`), halted
LightDM, and ran the installer script with DKMS module
registration, 32-bit compatibility libraries, and automatic X
configuration setup.
* Restarted Display Services [Verified]: Executed `sudo systemctl
start lightdm`.
* Verified Operational Status [Verified]: User explicitly confirmed:
*"Perfect back to normal everything works fine now"*. Dualmonitor rendering and 28" external display connectivity are
verified fully functional.
## 4. Current state
* Working:
* NVIDIA proprietary driver version 470.256.02 compiled and active
via DKMS on the active Linux kernel.
* External 28" monitor detection and desktop extension via the
Dell E-Port Replicator PR03X dock.
* LightDM display manager active and rendering full hardware
acceleration.
* Broken:
* None.
* Uncertain:
* None.
## 5. Decisions, preferences, and safeguards
* Accepted Solutions: Official NVIDIA binary installer `470.256.02`
compiled with DKMS registration for the legacy Quadro K2100M
card.
* System Safeguards:
* Do not install newer unvetted driver releases (e.g., NVIDIA 580
series branch), as they break compatibility with the underlying
hardware architecture (Quadro K2100M Kepler architecture).
* DKMS must remain configured to attempt automated
recompilation upon future minor kernel package updates.
* Operational Preference: Preserve terminal-based driver
deployment and avoid unnecessary GRUB kernel parameter
overrides when standard systemctl/runfile procedures succeed.
## 6. Next action and stopping conditions
* Recommended Action: **Leave the system untouched.**
* Stopping Condition: The system is in a fully working state and
meeting all performance and display requirements. No additional
changes, commands, or modifications are justified.
* Reopening Condition: Re-evaluate driver state only if a future
`apt upgrade` or major Linux Mint kernel revision breaks DKMS
auto-compilation, resulting in loss of display signal on the external
28" monitor.

3. One correction about the update process
There is an important distinction:
So apt-get update itself does not compile the kernel or install 580.
The actual question is: when did APT install the 580 packages, and which transaction
brought them in?
Command What it does
apt-get update Refreshes package indexes.
apt-get upgrade Installs available package updates, potentially including a kernel.
DKMS May automatically compile registered modules for newly installed kernels.

Good. This confirms that the 580 stack is essentially self-contained within the transitional
driver chain. We have not found an independent application or package that requires it.
There is one important detail in your output, though: apt-cache rdepends --installed
is not a clean list of installed reverse dependencies. It also displays candidate packages
from the repository, which explains the long list of AWS, Azure, GCP, generic and other kernel
variants. We must not interpret those as installed packages.
What the results tell us
nvidia-driver-535 is the direct installed reverse dependency keeping nvidiadriver-580 in place.
nvidia-utils-580 and the 580 graphics libraries belong to the 580 driver stack.
nvidia-dkms-535 is the transitional link to nvidia-dkms-580 .
nvidia-prime does not directly depend on the 580 driver. We should preserve it.
Nothing in this output indicates that your working, runfile-managed 470.256.02 module
needs the 580 stack.

Let's avoid another broad reverse-dependency listing. This command asks DPKG directly which
packages are installed and displays their dependency declarations.

place.

Excellent. We now have definitive evidence of the dependency chain, directly from the
installed package database.
1. Confirmed root cause
Your installed packages explicitly declare:
APT dependency chain
nvidia-driver-470-server
Held · Installed
Depends on
nvidia-driver-535
Automatic · Installed
Depends on
nvidia-driver-580
Automatic · Installed
The same chain exists for DKMS:
nvidia-dkms-470-server → nvidia-dkms-535 → nvidia-dkms-580
This is not a random collection of obsolete files. It is an installed package dependency
structure.
2. Important distinction: the working 470 installation
Your package inventory shows:
nvidia-dkms-470-server and nvidia-driver-470-server are installed.
Their package dependencies now point to 535, not to a self-contained 470 stack.
The actual working 470.256.02 DKMS source remains separately present under
/usr/src/nvidia-470.256.02 .
The active kernel module is still the 470.256.02 version, as previously verified.
We must therefore distinguish the APT package records from the working driver
installation. Removing a package is not automatically equivalent to removing the working
driver, but we must verify the exact effects before doing anything.
3. Proposed direction
I would investigate a controlled transition:
1. Replace the held transitional 470-server package pair with the regular Ubuntu 470
packages.
2. Preserve nvidia-prime and the unrelated EGL Wayland packages.
3. Remove the 535/580 dependency chain and its unused 580 userspace components.
4. Preserve the manually installed 470.256.02 DKMS source and verify its registration.
5. Check the proposed transaction for any removal or replacement of the working 470
module.
No purge, reinstall, package hold change, or DKMS modification is authorized yet.
4. Next step — simulate, do not install
Before constructing the transition, I want to confirm the exact package metadata for the
regular 470 packages and their candidates.

2. Two things deserve attention
A. The 580 removal is comprehensive.
APT proposes removing the 580 kernel source, firmware, utilities, libraries and Xorg driver.
This is what we want.
B. The 32 new packages include i386 graphics dependencies.
This is not necessarily a problem. Your current installation already has 32-bit NVIDIA libraries,
and the regular 470 driver recommends matching i386 libraries. However, we should
understand the additional Mesa and Vulkan dependencies rather than blindly accepting them.
3. The important unresolved risk: DKMS
The simulation does not tell us whether the new package's DKMS registration will safely
coexist with the existing runfile-managed 470.256.02 registration.
Both use the same module name and version. We should inspect the current registration and
source metadata before proceeding.
Run this read-only command:

PACKAGE_NAME="nvidia"

# By default, DKMS will add KERNELRELEASE to the make
command line; however,
# this will cause the kernel module build to infer that it was
invoked via
# Kbuild directly instead of DKMS. The dkms(8) manual page
recommends quoting
# the 'make' command name to suppress this behavior.
MAKE[0]="'make' -j8 NV_EXCLUDE_BUILD_MODULES=''
KERNEL_UNAME=${kernelver} IGNORE_CC_MISMATCH=''
modules"
# The list of kernel modules was generated by nvidia-installer at
runtime.

Prevent APT from replacing or unregistering that existing registration.
This may require a carefully constructed package transaction, and we should not force
dependencies or bypass package checks without understanding the consequences.

here the script to run secureupdate
#!/bin/bash
# ---------- TERMINAL COLORS ----------
GREEN='\033[0;32m'
RED='\033[0;31m'
YELLOW='\033[1;33m'
RESET='\033[0m'
green() { printf "${GREEN}✔ %s${RESET}\n" "$*"; }
red() { printf "${RED}✘ %s${RESET}\n" "$*"; }
yellow() { printf "${YELLOW}⚠ %s${RESET}\n" "$*"; }
# ---------- NVIDIA / KERNEL CHECK ----------
nvidia_check() {
local stage="$1"
local running_kernel latest_kernel
local loaded_version module_version dkms_status
running_kernel=$(uname -r)
echo
echo "========== NVIDIA / KERNEL CHECK: $stage
=========="
echo "Running kernel: $running_kernel"
# Check the currently loaded NVIDIA driver
if [[ ! -r /proc/driver/nvidia/version ]]; then
red "NVIDIA driver is not currently loaded."
return 1
fi
loaded_version=$(grep -m1 'NVIDIA UNIX' \
/proc/driver/nvidia/version |
grep -oE '[0-9]+\.[0-9]+\.[0-9]+' | head -n1)
if [[ "$loaded_version" != "470.256.02" ]]; then
red "Loaded NVIDIA version is ${loaded_version:-UNKNOWN},
not 470.256.02."
red "STOP: Review the NVIDIA driver state."
return 1
fi
green "Loaded NVIDIA driver 470.256.02 verified"
# Find the newest installed kernel
latest_kernel=$(find /lib/modules -mindepth 1 -maxdepth 1 \
-type d -printf '%f\n' | sort -V | tail -n1)
if [[ -z "$latest_kernel" ]]; then
red "No installed kernel module directories found."
return 1
fi
echo "Newest installed kernel: $latest_kernel"
if [[ "$latest_kernel" != "$running_kernel" ]]; then
yellow "Newest kernel differs from the running kernel."
yellow "A reboot may be needed after verifying the new
kernel."
fi
# Check NVIDIA module available for the newest installed kernel
module_version=$(modinfo -k "$latest_kernel" \
-F version nvidia 2>/dev/null)
if [[ "$module_version" != "470.256.02" ]]; then
red "NVIDIA module for $latest_kernel is ${module_version:-
NOT FOUND}."
red "Expected 470.256.02. STOP MAINTENANCE."
return 1
fi
green "NVIDIA 470.256.02 module verified for $latest_kernel"
# Verify DKMS registration for that specific kernel
dkms_status=$(dkms status 2>&1)
echo
echo "DKMS status:"
printf '%s\n' "$dkms_status"
if ! grep -F "nvidia/470.256.02, $latest_kernel," \
<<< "$dkms_status" | grep -q 'installed'; then
red "DKMS does not confirm NVIDIA 470.256.02 installed for
$latest_kernel."
red "STOP MAINTENANCE and review DKMS."
return 1
fi
green "DKMS registration verified for $latest_kernel"
}
# ---------- SYSTEM MAINTENANCE ----------
nvidia_check "BEFORE UPDATE" &&
sudo apt-get update &&
sudo apt-get upgrade &&

First, the secureupdate output is useful — and it reveals something important:
Nothing in that output indicates that my proposed removal was attempted. In fact, no
removal was performed in that maintenance run.
Your script is behaving consistently with its current checks, but those checks are not designed
to detect or remove the 580 branch.
Why 580 survives secureupdate
1. The NVIDIA check only verifies that 470 works. It confirms the loaded driver, module
version and DKMS registration for the newest kernel. It does not reject additional NVIDIA
branches.
2. APT upgrade does not remove packages simply because they are obsolete for your
GPU. The 580 packages remain installed and satisfy the transitional dependency chain.
3. Autoremove reports zero removals. That is expected because APT still sees the 580
packages as dependencies of the installed 535 and 470-server packages.
4. The 580 DKMS entries are still registered. Your output confirms both the old-kernel
installation and current-kernel build remain.
One correction to our earlier discussion: we have not executed a removal transaction. The
previous APT simulation was only a preview, and it proposed replacing the transitional 470-
server route with regular 470 packages.
So the next task is to inspect the real Ubuntu package archives and compare them with the
existing runfile-managed DKMS tree.
Read-only package inspection
This command:
Identifies the current APT candidate versions.
Downloads the three package archives into a temporary directory.
Extracts their contents without installing them.
Displays their maintainer scripts and DKMS configuration.
Leaves your existing NVIDIA installation untouched.

### preinst
#! /bin/sh
# preinst script for nvidia-dkms-470
#
# see: dh_installdeb(1)
#
# Copyright (C) 2017 Canonical Ltd
# Authors: Alberto Milone
set -e
# summary of how this script can be called:
# * <new-preinst> `install'
# * <new-preinst> `install' <old-version>
# * <new-preinst> `upgrade' <old-version>
# * <old-preinst> `abort-upgrade' <new-version>
#
# For details see /usr/share/doc/packaging-manual/
case "$1" in
install|upgrade)
# Attempt to remove a .run file installation of the driver before
installing the package
if [ -x /usr/bin/nvidia-uninstall ]; then
/usr/bin/nvidia-uninstall -s || :
fi;
# Attempt to stop the nvidia-persistenced process from the
currently
# installed package. This is needed to work-around the broken
postrm script
# introduced in 331.89-0ubuntu3. Without this, you can't
upgrade from, say,
# nvidia-331 to nvidia-343 (although you could still upgrade to a
newer
# nvidia-331 version/revision without error). For details, see:
# https://bugs.launchpad.net/ubuntu/+source/nvidia-graphicsdrivers-331/+bug/1394348
#if [ -e /usr/bin/stop-nvidia-persistenced ] && [ -x /usr/bin/stopnvidia-persistenced ]; then
# echo "Stopping previous nvidia-persistenced"
# /usr/bin/stop-nvidia-persistenced || true
# echo "Done."
#elif [ -e /lib/systemd/system/nvidia-persistenced.service ]; then
# if [ -x /bin/systemctl ]; then
# /bin/systemctl stop --no-block nvidia-persistenced || true
# fi
#fi
;;
abort-upgrade)
;;
*)
echo "preinst called with unknown argument \`$1'" >&2
exit 0
;;
esac
# dh_installdeb will replace this with shell code automatically
# generated by other debhelper scripts.
exit 0
### postinst
#!/bin/sh
# postinst script for nvidia-dkms-470
#
# see: dh_installdeb(1)
#
# Copyright (C) 2007-2008 Mario Limonciello
# Copyright (C) 2009 Canonical Ltd
# Authors: Alberto Milone
set -e
PACKAGE_NAME=nvidia
CVERSION=470.256.02
CVERSION=${CVERSION#*really.}
ARCH=dpkg --print-architecture
case $ARCH in
amd64)
ARCH="x86_64"
;;
i386)
ARCH="i686"
;;
armhf)
ARCH="armhf"
;;
*)
echo "WARNING: unsupported arch: $ARCH"
ARCH="$ARCH"
;;
esac
# Check the existence of a kernel named as $1
_is_kernel_name_correct() {
CORRECT="no"
KERNEL_NAME=$1
for kernel in /boot/config-*; do
[ -f "$kernel" ] || continue
KERNEL=${kernel#*-}
if [ "${KERNEL}" = "${KERNEL_NAME}" ]; then
CORRECT="yes"
break
fi
done
echo $CORRECT
}
# Get the most recent kernel on Debian based systems. This keeps
# into account both the version and the ABI. If the current kernel
# is the most recent kernel then the function will print a null string.
_get_newest_kernel_debian() {
NEWEST_KERNEL=
NEWEST_VERSION=
NEWEST_ABI=
for kernel in /boot/config-*; do
[ -f "$kernel" ] || continue
KERNEL=${kernel#*-}
KERNEL_VERSION=${KERNEL%-*}
ABI=${KERNEL#*-}
ABI=${ABI%-*}
if [ -z "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ]; then
# The 1st time get a version which is bigger than $1
COMPARE_TO=$1
else
# Get the biggest version
COMPARE_TO="$NEWEST_VERSION-$NEWEST_ABI"
fi
# if $kernel is greater than $COMPARE_TO
if [ dpkg --compare-versions
"$KERNEL_VERSION-$ABI" gt "$COMPARE_TO" && echo
"yes" || echo "no" = "yes" ]; then
NEWEST_KERNEL=$KERNEL
NEWEST_VERSION=$KERNEL_VERSION
NEWEST_ABI=$ABI
fi
done
echo "$NEWEST_KERNEL"
}
get_newest_kernel() {
NEWEST_KERNEL=
# Try Debian first as rpm can be installed in Debian based distros
if [ -e /usr/bin/dpkg ]; then
# If DEB based
CURRENT_KERNEL=$1
CURRENT_VERSION=${CURRENT_KERNEL%-*}
CURRENT_ABI=${CURRENT_KERNEL#*-}
CURRENT_FLAVOUR=${CURRENT_ABI#*-}
CURRENT_ABI=${CURRENT_ABI%-*}
NEWEST_KERNEL=$(_get_newest_kernel_debian
"$CURRENT_VERSION-$CURRENT_ABI")
fi
# Make sure that kernel name that we extracted corresponds to
an installed
# kernel
if [ -n "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ] && [ _is_kernel_name_correct
$NEWEST_KERNEL = "no" ]; then
NEWEST_KERNEL=
fi
echo $NEWEST_KERNEL
}
case "$1" in
configure)
# Deal with alternatives
# Clean up any previous non-multi-arch alternatives
if [ -n "$(update-alternatives --list gl_conf 2>/dev/null)" ]; then
set -a $(update-alternatives --list gl_conf 2>/dev/null)
while [ $# -ge 1 ] && [ "$#" != "configure" ]; do
ALTERNATIVE=${1}
update-alternatives --remove gl_conf $ALTERNATIVE
shift
done
fi
CURRENT_KERNEL=$(uname -r)
NEWEST_KERNEL=$(get_newest_kernel
"$CURRENT_KERNEL")
# Update initramfs so that the blacklist ends up in the
initramfs
if [ -x /usr/sbin/update-initramfs ]; then
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u
if [ -n "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ] && [ ${CURRENT_KERNEL} !=
${NEWEST_KERNEL} ]; then
# This can fail when dealing with a chroot see LP: #556653
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u -k $CURRENT_KERNEL
fi
fi
# Enable any quirks for the driver
if [ which quirks-handler ]; then
quirks-handler -e $PACKAGE_NAME -v
fi
# Build the kernel module
/usr/lib/dkms/common.postinst $PACKAGE_NAME $CVERSION
/usr/share/$PACKAGE_NAME $ARCH $2
exit $?
;;
abort-upgrade|abort-remove|abort-deconfigure)
;;
*)
echo "postinst called with unknown argument \`$1'" >&2
exit 1
;;
esac
### prerm
#!/bin/sh
# prerm script for nvidia-dkms-470
#
# Copyright (C) 2007 Mario Limonciello
# Copyright (C) 2009 Canonical Ltd
# Authors: Alberto Milone
PACKAGE_NAME=nvidia
CVERSION=470.256.02
CVERSION=${CVERSION#*really.}
remove_dkms_module() {
echo "Removing all DKMS Modules"
dkms remove -m $PACKAGE_NAME -v $CVERSION --all >
/dev/null
echo "Done."
}
case "$1" in
upgrade)
remove_dkms_module
;;
remove)
remove_dkms_module
# Remove any quirks for the driver
if [ which quirks-handler ]; then
quirks-handler -d $PACKAGE_NAME -v
fi
;;
esac
### postrm
#! /bin/sh
# postrm script for nvidia-dkms-470
#
# see: dh_installdeb(1)
#
# Copyright (C) 2018 Canonical Ltd
# Authors: Alberto Milone
set -e
# summary of how this script can be called:
# * <postrm> `remove'
# * <postrm> `purge'
# * <old-postrm> `upgrade' <new-version>
# * <new-postrm> `failed-upgrade' <old-version>
# * <new-postrm> `abort-install'
# * <new-postrm> `abort-install' <old-version>
# * <new-postrm> `abort-upgrade' <old-version>
# * <disappearer's-postrm> `disappear' <r>overwrit>r> <newversion>
# for details, see /usr/share/doc/packaging-manual/
case "$1" in
remove|purge)
# This should make sure that #PKGLIBDIR# and
# #PKGLIBDIR32# are removed even if some
# links are left. See LP: #540143
rm -Rf #PKGLIBDIR# #PKGLIBDIR32#
# Remove the blacklist file for hybrid graphics
rm -f /etc/modprobe.d/nvidia-470_hybrid.conf
# Update initramfs, in case we were loaded into it
if [ -x /usr/sbin/update-initramfs ]; then
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u
fi
;;
upgrade|failed-upgrade|abort-install|abort-upgrade|disappear)
;;
*)
echo "postrm called with unknown argument \`$1'" >&2
exit 0
;;
esac

# generated by other debhelper scripts.

### postinst
#!/bin/sh
# postinst script for nvidia-kernel-common-470
#
# see: dh_installdeb(1)
#
# Copyright (C) 2007-2008 Mario Limonciello
# Copyright (C) 2009 Canonical Ltd
# Authors: Alberto Milone
set -e
# Check the existence of a kernel named as $1
_is_kernel_name_correct() {
CORRECT="no"
KERNEL_NAME=$1
for kernel in /boot/config-*; do
[ -f "$kernel" ] || continue
KERNEL=${kernel#*-}
if [ "${KERNEL}" = "${KERNEL_NAME}" ]; then
CORRECT="yes"
break
fi
done
echo $CORRECT
}
# Get the most recent kernel on Debian based systems. This keeps
# into account both the version and the ABI. If the current kernel
# is the most recent kernel then the function will print a null string.
_get_newest_kernel_debian() {
NEWEST_KERNEL=
NEWEST_VERSION=
NEWEST_ABI=
for kernel in /boot/config-*; do
[ -f "$kernel" ] || continue
KERNEL=${kernel#*-}
KERNEL_VERSION=${KERNEL%-*}
ABI=${KERNEL#*-}
ABI=${ABI%-*}
if [ -z "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ]; then
# The 1st time get a version which is bigger than $1
COMPARE_TO=$1
else
# Get the biggest version
COMPARE_TO="$NEWEST_VERSION-$NEWEST_ABI"
fi
# if $kernel is greater than $COMPARE_TO
if [ dpkg --compare-versions
"$KERNEL_VERSION-$ABI" gt "$COMPARE_TO" && echo
"yes" || echo "no" = "yes" ]; then
NEWEST_KERNEL=$KERNEL
NEWEST_VERSION=$KERNEL_VERSION
NEWEST_ABI=$ABI
fi
done
echo "$NEWEST_KERNEL"
}
get_newest_kernel() {
NEWEST_KERNEL=
# Try Debian first as rpm can be installed in Debian based distros
if [ -e /usr/bin/dpkg ]; then
# If DEB based
CURRENT_KERNEL=$1
CURRENT_VERSION=${CURRENT_KERNEL%-*}
CURRENT_ABI=${CURRENT_KERNEL#*-}
CURRENT_FLAVOUR=${CURRENT_ABI#*-}
CURRENT_ABI=${CURRENT_ABI%-*}
NEWEST_KERNEL=$(_get_newest_kernel_debian
"$CURRENT_VERSION-$CURRENT_ABI")
fi
# Make sure that kernel name that we extracted corresponds to
an installed
# kernel
if [ -n "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ] && [ _is_kernel_name_correct
$NEWEST_KERNEL = "no" ]; then
NEWEST_KERNEL=
fi
echo $NEWEST_KERNEL
}
case "$1" in
configure)
CURRENT_KERNEL=$(uname -r)
NEWEST_KERNEL=$(get_newest_kernel
"$CURRENT_KERNEL")
# Update initramfs so that the blacklist ends up in the
initramfs
if [ -x /usr/sbin/update-initramfs ]; then
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u
if [ -n "$NEWEST_KERNEL" ] && [ ${CURRENT_KERNEL} !=
${NEWEST_KERNEL} ]; then
# This can fail when dealing with a chroot see LP: #556653
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u -k $CURRENT_KERNEL
fi
fi
;;
abort-upgrade|abort-remove|abort-deconfigure)
;;
*)
echo "postinst called with unknown argument \`$1'" >&2
exit 1
;;
esac
# Automatically added by dh_systemd_enable/13.14.1ubuntu5
if [ "$1" = "configure" ] || [ "$1" = "abort-upgrade" ] || [ "$1" = "abortdeconfigure" ] || [ "$1" = "abort-remove" ] ; then
# The following line should be removed in trixie or trixie+1
deb-systemd-helper unmask 'nvidia-hibernate.service' >/dev/null
|| true
# was-enabled defaults to true, so new installations run enable.
if deb-systemd-helper --quiet was-enabled 'nvidiahibernate.service'; then
# Enables the unit on first installation, creates new
# symlinks on upgrades if the unit file has changed.
deb-systemd-helper enable 'nvidia-hibernate.service'
>/dev/null || true
else
# Update the statefile to add new symlinks (if any), which need
to be
# cleaned up on purge. Also remove old symlinks.
deb-systemd-helper update-state 'nvidia-hibernate.service'
>/dev/null || true
fi
fi
# End automatically added section
# Automatically added by dh_systemd_enable/13.14.1ubuntu5
if [ "$1" = "configure" ] || [ "$1" = "abort-upgrade" ] || [ "$1" = "abortdeconfigure" ] || [ "$1" = "abort-remove" ] ; then

set -e
# summary of how this script can be called:
# * <postrm> `remove'
# * <postrm> `purge'
# * <old-postrm> `upgrade' <new-version>
# * <new-postrm> `failed-upgrade' <old-version>
# * <new-postrm> `abort-install'
# * <new-postrm> `abort-install' <old-version>
# * <new-postrm> `abort-upgrade' <old-version>
# * <disappearer's-postrm> `disappear' <r>overwrit>r> <newversion>
# for details, see /usr/share/doc/packaging-manual/
case "$1" in
remove|purge)
# Remove the blacklist file for hybrid graphics
rm -f /etc/modprobe.d/nvidia-470_hybrid.conf
# Update initramfs, in case we were loaded into it
if [ -x /usr/sbin/update-initramfs ]; then
/usr/sbin/update-initramfs -u
fi
;;
upgrade|failed-upgrade|abort-install|abort-upgrade|disappear)
;;
*)
echo "postrm called with unknown argument \`$1'" >&2
exit 0
;;
esac

# generated by other debhelper scripts.

This inspection uncovered the most important issue so far.
Important DKMS conflict risk confirmed
The Ubuntu package's nvidia-dkms-470 maintainer scripts use the same DKMS identity as
your existing runfile installation:
And its pre-install script explicitly attempts to remove an existing NVIDIA .run installation:
Its post-install script then registers/builds the packaged source:
Its removal script also removes all DKMS registrations for that identity:
That means we must not casually install or remove nvidia-dkms-470 while the current
runfile-managed 470 installation is active. An ordinary package transition could uninstall the
working runfile installation and alter the DKMS registration we are trying to preserve.
There is another important detail: the source archive has not yet been compared with your
existing 49 MB source tree. The package's dkms.conf was extracted separately from the
source archive, so the next step is to compare both configurations and their file inventories.

Important differences
The Ubuntu package also includes a CLEAN command and explicit build settings.
2. The uninstaller is present
This is particularly important:
The Ubuntu package's pre-install script explicitly invokes it.
Therefore, installing the packaged DKMS driver is not a harmless way to replace the existing
DKMS configuration. It can first uninstall your current runfile installation.
My recommendation: do not install the package yet.
3. What I recommend investigating next

registered on your system and which files it manages.

What we can conclude
1. The runfile installer database is present and identifies version 470.256.02.
2. Its recorded file mappings include NVIDIA 580 library filenames.
3. The database has therefore been affected by the coexistence of multiple NVIDIA
installation branches, or by subsequent changes to files registered under those paths.
4. We cannot yet conclude that the runfile installer itself installed 580. APT may have
replaced files at paths previously registered by the runfile installer.
That last distinction matters: the database may contain stale or conflicting records. We should
not invoke nvidia-uninstall until we understand them.

Its timestamp, October 20 at 20:07, matches the recorded 470 installation activity.
This is strong evidence that the current NVVM library belongs to the later runfile-managed
installation state.
2. What the installer database reveals
The manifest is not a simple list of files belonging to one clean driver version.
It contains:

A later 470 library set.
Records of symbolic links being redirected between different versioned libraries.
Both historical and current-looking file entries.
This explains why relying on the installer database header alone would be misleading.

Excellent, both reports are useful. I've inspected them. The diagnostic script did its job; the
backup script has a genuine permissions-handling bug. We can fix that without touching the
NVIDIA installation.
1. Diagnostic report — successful
470.256.02 confirmed operational
The November 1 report confirms:
One additional observation: all three NVIDIA suspend-related services are enabled but
inactive:
That is a useful lead for our separate S3 investigation, but it does not establish that these
services caused the resume problem.
2. Backup failure — identified
Script bug
Check Result
GPU Quadro K2100M
Running kernel 6.8.0-147-generic
Loaded driver 470.256.02
nvidia-smi Working
External display NVIDIA reports display active
470 DKMS Installed for current kernel
580 DKMS Still present
535/580 APT packages Still installed
nvidia-suspend.service enabled / inactive
nvidia-resume.service enabled / inactive
nvidia-hibernate.service enabled / inactive
The problem is in how I designed the backup staging process.
The original script effectively did this:
Two related failures occurred:
1. tar ran as your normal user and couldn't read the root-protected installer database.
2. The cleanup trap ran rm -rf as your normal user, but the copied NVIDIA source files and
configuration files were root-owned.
That explains both the initial tar error and the long sequence of rm: Permission denied
messages.
This is my script-design mistake, not a problem with your sudo privileges.
3. What about the failed backup's leftovers?
The log shows that the temporary directory was not completely cleaned up:
A partial archive may also have been created.
Let's not delete anything yet. First, we should inspect the exact leftovers, their ownership and
whether an archive exists.
Normal user
|
v
Create temporary staging directory
|
v
sudo cp -a protected system files
|
v
Files copied with root ownership
|
v
Normal user runs tar
|
X
Permission denied
/home/laco/Documents/NVIDIA-Recovery/backups/
└── .stage-20261101-161105.b8pDFH/
4. How I propose fixing it
The corrected backup script should use a different architecture:
Corrected backup workflow
Normal user
Creates a user-owned temporary staging directory and collects diagnostic metadata.
sudo tar
Reads protected system paths directly and streams the archive to a file created by your
user.
Normal user
Verifies archive integrity, applies restrictive permissions and removes its own temporary
staging directory.
This avoids copying root-owned files into a user-owned temporary directory in the first place.
5. Next step
I recommend two small steps:
1. Inspect the failed backup's leftovers without modifying anything.
2. Replace nvidia-backup.sh with the corrected version and test it.

I've reviewed the complete script. We have identified the exact cause of the failure, and there
are a few additional weaknesses worth correcting before another backup attempt.
1. Script audit
Current script is not safe to rerun
The underlying mistake is clear: the script mixes privileged copying with unprivileged
archiving and cleanup.
Lines Problem Consequence
14–
15
Creates user-owned staging, then uses normal rm
in EXIT trap
Root-owned copied files prevent cleanup
27–
29
sudo cp -a into staging Preserves root ownership and restrictive
permissions
41 Runs tar without sudo Cannot read protected staged files
41 Writes directly to final archive Failed runs can leave misleading partial
archives
42 chmod happens after tar Partial archive can remain if tar fails
45–
46
Listing check is not a strict integrity test Pipeline may not reliably detect archive
errors
2. Corrected architecture
I recommend changing the design rather than adding more sudo commands to the existing
script.
Proposed backup workflow
Normal user
Create private staging directory and collect diagnostic metadata
Root tar process
Read protected source paths directly and stream archive data
Normal user
Receive temporary archive, verify integrity, set permissions
Verified archive
Atomic rename to final .tar.gz
This avoids putting root-owned files inside the user's staging directory.
3. Additional safeguards I want included
sudo -v before starting privileged work.
A unique temporary archive in the backup directory.
No final archive name until verification succeeds.
tar -tzf with strict error handling.
Cleanup restricted to temporary artifacts created by the current execution.
Preserve archive permissions ( 600 ).
Record missing source paths rather than silently ignoring them.
Keep the optional secureupdate argument.
Preserve original ownership and permissions of backed-up system files inside the archive.
No restoration or modification functionality.

ChatGPT said:
One important detail: the archive will contain sensitive system configuration and NVIDIA
installer state. It should remain private.

protected system paths directly while generating the archive; it will not copy root-owned files
into staging.

archiving or verification fails, it removes only temporary files created by that run.
Step 1 — Install the corrected script
Copy this entire block into your Linux Mint terminal. It first builds and syntax-checks a
temporary replacement, then preserves the original and installs the new version atomically.
set -Eeuo pipefail
SCRIPT_DIR="/home/laco/Documents/NVIDIA-Recovery/scripts"
SCRIPT="$SCRIPT_DIR/nvidia-backup.sh"
STAMP="$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
ORIGINAL_BACKUP="$SCRIPT.pre-fix-$STAMP"
NEW_SCRIPT="$(mktemp "$SCRIPT_DIR/.nvidia-backup.sh.new.XXXXXX")"
trap 'rm -f -- "$NEW_SCRIPT"' EXIT
if [ ! -f "$SCRIPT" ]; then
echo "ERROR: original script not found"
exit 1
fi
if [ -e "$ORIGINAL_BACKUP" ]; then
echo "ERROR: preservation filename already exists"
exit 1
fi
cat > "$NEW_SCRIPT" <<'SCRIPT_EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Targeted NVIDIA backup.
# Reads protected paths using sudo; does not restore or modify them.
# Usage: bash nvidia-backup.sh [optional-exact-path-to-secureupdate]
set -Eeuo pipefail
umask 077
SCRIPT_DIR="$(cd -- "$(dirname -- "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)"
PROJECT_DIR="$(cd -- "$SCRIPT_DIR/.." && pwd)"
BACKUP_DIR="$PROJECT_DIR/backups"
mkdir -p "$BACKUP_DIR"
for cmd in sudo tar gzip mktemp date hostname dkms dpkg-query apt-mark
modinfo; do
command -v "$cmd" >/dev/null 2>&1 || {
echo "Required command missing: $cmd" >&2
exit 1
}
done
OPTIONAL="${1:-}"
if [ -n "$OPTIONAL" ] && [ ! -f "$OPTIONAL" ]; then
echo "Not a regular file: $OPTIONAL" >&2
exit 1
fi
# Authenticate before creating backup artifacts.
sudo -v
STAMP="$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
STAGE="$(mktemp -d "$BACKUP_DIR/.stage-$STAMP.XXXXXX")"
TMP_ARCHIVE=""
ARCHIVE="$BACKUP_DIR/nvidia-recovery-$STAMP.tar.gz"
cleanup() {
local rc=$?
if [ -n "$TMP_ARCHIVE" ] && [ -e "$TMP_ARCHIVE" ]; then
rm -f -- "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
fi
if [ -n "$STAGE" ] && [ -d "$STAGE" ]; then
rm -rf -- "$STAGE"
fi
exit "$rc"
}
trap cleanup EXIT
if [ -e "$ARCHIVE" ]; then
echo "Archive already exists; refusing to overwrite: $ARCHIVE" >&2
exit 1
fi
mkdir -p "$STAGE/system-state" "$STAGE/config"
echo "Collecting system state..."
uname -a > "$STAGE/system-state/uname.txt"
cat /proc/cmdline > "$STAGE/system-state/proc-cmdline.txt" 2>/dev/null ||
true
dkms status > "$STAGE/system-state/dkms-status.txt" 2>&1 || true
apt-mark showhold > "$STAGE/system-state/apt-holds.txt" 2>&1 || true
dpkg-query -W -f='${db:Status-Abbrev} ${binary:Package} ${Version}\n'
2>/dev/null \
| grep -Ei 'nvidia|libnvidia' \
> "$STAGE/system-state/nvidia-packages.txt" || true
cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version \
> "$STAGE/system-state/loaded-nvidia-version.txt" 2>&1 || true
modinfo -F version nvidia \
> "$STAGE/system-state/modinfo-version.txt" 2>&1 || true
modinfo -n nvidia \
> "$STAGE/system-state/modinfo-path.txt" 2>&1 || true
echo "Checking selected protected paths..."
SYSTEM_PATHS=()
for p in \
etc/modprobe.d \
etc/X11/xorg.conf \
etc/default/grub \
var/lib/nvidia \
usr/src/nvidia-470.256.02
do
if sudo test -e "/$p"; then
SYSTEM_PATHS+=("$p")
else
echo "Not present: /$p" >> "$STAGE/system-state/missing-paths.txt"
fi
done
if [ -n "$OPTIONAL" ]; then
cp -p -- "$OPTIONAL" "$STAGE/config/secureupdate"
fi
cat > "$STAGE/README.txt" <<EOF
Targeted NVIDIA recovery backup
Created: $(date --iso-8601=seconds 2>/dev/null || date)
Host: $(hostname)
This is a preservation snapshot, not an automated restore package.
System paths are archived directly with elevated read access.
Numeric ownership and file permissions are preserved.
Do not blindly restore the installer database or run the NVIDIA uninstaller.
Review docs/03-backup-and-recovery-plan.md before recovery.
EOF
echo "Preparing temporary archive..."
TMP_ARCHIVE="$(mktemp "$BACKUP_DIR/.nvidia-recovery-$STAMP.XXXXXX.tmp")"
# Root reads protected paths directly.
# The normal user's shell creates and owns the archive file.
sudo tar --create --gzip --file=- --numeric-owner \
-C "$STAGE" . \
-C / "${SYSTEM_PATHS[@]}" \
> "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
chmod 600 "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
echo "Verifying archive integrity..."
tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" > /dev/null
echo "Archive listing (first 100 entries):"
tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" | sed -n '1,100p'
# Publish only after successful integrity verification.
mv -- "$TMP_ARCHIVE" "$ARCHIVE"
TMP_ARCHIVE=""
echo
echo "Backup created: $ARCHIVE"
echo "Archive size: $(du -h "$ARCHIVE" | awk '{print $1}')"
echo "Permissions: $(stat -c '%a' "$ARCHIVE")"
echo "Backup complete. No driver/package/configuration changes performed."
SCRIPT_EOF
chmod 755 "$NEW_SCRIPT"
echo "=== CHECK NEW SCRIPT SYNTAX ==="
bash -n "$NEW_SCRIPT"
echo "=== PRESERVE ORIGINAL SCRIPT ==="
cp -p -- "$SCRIPT" "$ORIGINAL_BACKUP"
echo "=== INSTALL CORRECTED SCRIPT ==="
mv -f -- "$NEW_SCRIPT" "$SCRIPT"
echo
echo "=== VERIFY INSTALLED SCRIPT ==="
stat -c '%n | owner=%U:%G | mode=%a | size=%s bytes' "$SCRIPT"
sha256sum "$SCRIPT"
echo

echo "=== PRESERVED ORIGINAL ==="
stat -c '%n | owner=%U:%G | mode=%a | size=%s bytes' "$ORIGINAL_BACKUP"
echo
echo "=== FINAL SYNTAX CHECK ==="
bash -n "$SCRIPT" && echo "PASS"

1#!/usr/bin/env bash
2# Targeted NVIDIA backup.
3# Reads protected paths using sudo; does not restore or
modify them.
4# Usage: bash nvidia-backup.sh [optional-exact-path-tosecureupdate]
5
6set -Eeuo pipefail
7umask 077
8
9SCRIPT_DIR="$(cd -- "$(dirname -- "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" &&
pwd)"
10 PROJECT_DIR="$(cd -- "$SCRIPT_DIR/.." && pwd)"
11 BACKUP_DIR="$PROJECT_DIR/backups"
12
13 mkdir -p "$BACKUP_DIR"
14
15 for cmd in sudo tar gzip mktemp date hostname dkms dpkgquery apt-mark modinfo; do
16 command -v "$cmd" >/dev/null 2>&1 || {
17 echo "Required command missing: $cmd" >&2
18 exit 1
19 }
20 done
21
22 OPTIONAL="${1:-}"
23
24 if [ -n "$OPTIONAL" ] && [ ! -f "$OPTIONAL" ]; then
25 echo "Not a regular file: $OPTIONAL" >&2
26 exit 1
27 fi
28
29 # Authenticate before creating backup artifacts.
30 sudo -v
31
32 STAMP="$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
33 STAGE="$(mktemp -d
"$BACKUP_DIR/.stage-$STAMP.XXXXXX")"
34 TMP_ARCHIVE=""
35 ARCHIVE="$BACKUP_DIR/nvidia-recovery-$STAMP.tar.gz"
36
37 cleanup() {
38 local rc=$?
39
40 if [ -n "$TMP_ARCHIVE" ] && [ -e "$TMP_ARCHIVE" ]; then
41 rm -f -- "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
42 fi
43
44 if [ -n "$STAGE" ] && [ -d "$STAGE" ]; then
45 rm -rf -- "$STAGE"
46 fi
47
48 exit "$rc"
49 }
50 trap cleanup EXIT
51
52 if [ -e "$ARCHIVE" ]; then
53 echo "Archive already exists; refusing to overwrite:
$ARCHIVE" >&2
54 exit 1
55 fi
56
57 mkdir -p "$STAGE/system-state" "$STAGE/config"
58
59 echo "Collecting system state..."
60
61 uname -a > "$STAGE/system-state/uname.txt"
62 cat /proc/cmdline > "$STAGE/system-state/proc-cmdline.txt"
2>/dev/null || true
63 dkms status > "$STAGE/system-state/dkms-status.txt" 2>&1
|| true
64 apt-mark showhold > "$STAGE/system-state/apt-holds.txt"
2>&1 || true
65
66 dpkg-query -W -f='${db:Status-Abbrev} ${binary:Package}
${Version}\n' 2>/dev/null \
67 | grep -Ei 'nvidia|libnvidia' \
68 > "$STAGE/system-state/nvidia-packages.txt" || true
69
70 cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version \
71 > "$STAGE/system-state/loaded-nvidia-version.txt" 2>&1 ||
true
72
73 modinfo -F version nvidia \
74 > "$STAGE/system-state/modinfo-version.txt" 2>&1 || true
75
76 modinfo -n nvidia \
77 > "$STAGE/system-state/modinfo-path.txt" 2>&1 || true
78
79 echo "Checking selected protected paths..."
80
81 SYSTEM_PATHS=()
82
83 for p in \
84 etc/modprobe.d \
85 etc/X11/xorg.conf \
86 etc/default/grub \
87 var/lib/nvidia \
88 usr/src/nvidia-470.256.02
89 do
90 if sudo test -e "/$p"; then
91 SYSTEM_PATHS+=("$p")
92 else
93 echo "Not present: /$p" >> "$STAGE/systemstate/missing-paths.txt"
94 fi
95 done
96
97 if [ -n "$OPTIONAL" ]; then
98 cp -p -- "$OPTIONAL" "$STAGE/config/secureupdate"
99 fi
100
101 cat > "$STAGE/README.txt" <<EOF
102 Targeted NVIDIA recovery backup
103 Created: $(date --iso-8601=seconds 2>/dev/null || date)
104 Host: $(hostname)
105
106 This is a preservation snapshot, not an automated restore
package.
107 System paths are archived directly with elevated read access.
108 Numeric ownership and file permissions are preserved.
109
110 Do not blindly restore the installer database or run the
NVIDIA uninstaller.
111 Review docs/03-backup-and-recovery-plan.md before
recovery.
112 EOF
113
114 echo "Preparing temporary archive..."
115
116 TMP_ARCHIVE="$(mktemp "$BACKUP_DIR/.nvidiarecovery-$STAMP.XXXXXX.tmp")"
117
118 # Root reads protected paths directly.
119 # The normal user's shell creates and owns the archive file.
120 sudo tar --create --gzip --file=- --numeric-owner \
121 -C "$STAGE" . \
122 -C / "${SYSTEM_PATHS[@]}" \
123 > "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
124
125 chmod 600 "$TMP_ARCHIVE"
126
127 echo "Verifying archive integrity..."
128
129 tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" > /dev/null
130
131 echo "Archive listing (first 100 entries):"
132 tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" | sed -n '1,100p'
133
134 # Publish only after successful integrity verification.
135 mv -- "$TMP_ARCHIVE" "$ARCHIVE"
136 TMP_ARCHIVE=""
137
138 echo
139 echo "Backup created: $ARCHIVE"
140 echo "Archive size: $(du -h "$ARCHIVE" | awk '{print $1}')"
141 echo "Permissions: $(stat -c '%a' "$ARCHIVE")"
142 echo "Backup complete. No driver/package/configuration
changes performed."

The review confirms that the replacement is installed correctly, GNU tar 1.35 supports all the
metadata options we need, and the backup directory is clean.
There is one important improvement before execution: I want to add ACL and extendedattribute preservation to the archive. These can carry file permissions and security metadata
that ordinary mode bits do not capture.
There is also a verification detail: the script currently lists the archive twice, and its second
listing uses a pipeline whose error handling can be less reliable than a direct integrity check.
I'll simplify that and make the listing part of the same verified pass.

import pathlib
import sys
source = pathlib.Path(sys.argv[1])
destination = pathlib.Path(sys.argv[2])
text = source.read_text()
replacements = [
(
"Numeric ownership and file permissions are preserved.",
"Numeric ownership, file permissions, POSIX ACLs, extended
attributes, "
"and available SELinux contexts are preserved."
),
(
"sudo tar --create --gzip --file=- --numeric-owner \\",
"sudo tar --create --gzip --file=- --numeric-owner --acls --xattrs -
-selinux \\"
),
(
'echo "Verifying archive integrity..."\n\n'
'tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" > /dev/null\n\n'
'echo "Archive listing (first 100 entries):"\n'
'tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" | sed -n \'1,100p\'',
'echo "Verifying archive integrity and generating listing..."\n\n'
'LISTING="$STAGE/archive-listing.txt"\n'
'tar -tzf "$TMP_ARCHIVE" > "$LISTING"\n\n'
'echo "Archive listing (first 100 entries):"\n'
'sed -n \'1,100p\' "$LISTING"'
)
]
for old, new in replacements:
count = text.count(old)
if count != 1:
raise SystemExit(
f"Refusing edit: expected one occurrence, found {count}:
{old!r}"
)
text = text.replace(old, new, 1)
destination.write_text(text)
PY

mv -- "$TEMP" "$SCRIPT"
